Study participants

The study aimed to recruit 12 adult patients with chronic primary pain (CPP). For qualitative outcomes, such as feasibility and acceptance, thematic saturation is expected to be achieved with a sample size of approximately 12 participants (Guest et al., 2006; Turner-Bowker et al., 2018). To evaluate within-person effects, a sample size of n = 1 is sufficient because each individual is analyzed separately. Inclusion criteria were: age ≥ 18 years, access to a compatible smartphone (Android ≥ 5.1 or iOS ≥ 11.0), a primary diagnosis of CPP (ICD-10: F45.4x), and subjective pain-related disability. Exclusion criteria comprised acute comorbid conditions requiring immediate treatment (e.g., substance abuse, psychosis, severe underweight, or suicidality), migraine, headache, or neck pain as the primary complaint, insufficient German language proficiency, illiteracy, and physical inability to attend regular outpatient sessions. Diagnoses and comorbidities were assessed using the German brief version of the Diagnostic Interview for Mental Disorders (Mini-DIPS; Margraf & Cwik, 2020). German language proficiency was assessed via self-report (5-point Likert scale), with scores ≥ 4 indicating insufficient proficiency. Concurrent participation in other psychotherapy studies or ongoing psychotherapy was not permitted.

Measures

All assessments were conducted using m-Path (Mestdagh et al., 2022). M-Path is a research platform that is flexible and easy to use. It is used for ambulatory assessment and intensive longitudinal data collection.

EMA items

Psychological processes were assessed with the German ProCEss item set, developed for the EMA-based assessment of key processes in chronic pain (V. E. Hofmann, Glombiewski, Heider, et al., 2026) and grounded in established models of chronic pain and recommended core outcome domains (Dworkin et al., 2005). Its 25 items cover 14 processes, including pain-related worry, avoidance, depressive symptoms, acceptance, values-based behavior, expectations, thought suppression, task persistence, self-efficacy, self-compassion, positive and negative affect, pain intensity, and pain-related disability. Items refer to the period since the last assessment and are rated from 0 (not at all) to 10 (completely). The disability item ("How much did you feel disabled because of your pain?") served as the EMAbased disability outcome. Patients and clinicians rated the item set as content-valid and clinically useful, and psychometric evaluation indicated adequate within-person variability and generally satisfactory reliability, with lower reliability for self-efficacy, self-compassion, and particularly thought suppression (V. E. Hofmann, Glombiewski, Heider, et al., 2026).

Feasibility and acceptability

Patients and therapists completed adapted versions of the System Usability Scale (Gao et al., 2020) and the User Experience Questionnaire -Short Version (UEQ-S; Schrepp et al., 2017) to evaluate the usability of the m-Path app and the associated presentation of the study results. (Details regarding SUS and UEQ are reported in the Supplementary Material S1.3 Measures, Feasibility Outcomes). Feasibility was evaluated via compliance rates with the assessment protocol across study phases. A compliance rate of ≥ 80% was considered indicative of good feasibility.

Acceptance was assessed using qualitative interviews, quantitative questionnaires, and compliance rates. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with patients and therapists as the final assessment of the study to explore expectations, usability, and perceived utility of the intervention components. Core domains included expectations, study onboarding, app usability, assessment burden (e.g., EMA frequency), feasibility of the study procedure, perceived utility of the intervention components, and potential negative effects. Interviews also addressed the comprehensibility and usefulness of person-specific networks and the acceptance of algorithm-guided intervention selection. Therapists were additionally asked about the clarity and clinical utility of the presentation of the results.

Intervention

Treatment planning using an algorithm-guided decision process

Each participant was assigned to one of ten intervention modules in a structured team procedure, the Algorithm-Guided Intervention Selection Team (AGIST; Figure 2). First, the study therapist, supervisor, and researcher developed a shared case conceptualization from the diagnostic information without knowledge of the network results and documented the modules they considered suitable. The researcher then presented the network-derived treatment target (the variable with the highest mean of contemporaneous strength and temporal out-strength; Rubel et al., 2018) and the two modules recommended by a predefined matching matrix (V. E. Hofmann, Glombiewski, & Scholten, 2026). The primary recommendation was adopted unless adverse effects were anticipated, in which case the alternative module was chosen. The selected module was discussed with the patient and delivered throughout the intervention phase; reassignment in supervision was possible in case of adverse effects or sustained non-response.

---Please insert Figure 2 about here ----

Intervention modules

The ten modules were derived from cognitive-behavioral and third-wave treatments for chronic pain and adapted from established treatment manuals: exposure in vivo, relaxation, activity pacing, cognitive coping, attention control, resource activation, acceptance, values-based action, sleep, and self-compassion (see S1.1, Table S1). After one or two sessions of psychoeducation and motivational support, depending on patients' prior knowledge, each module followed the same four-part sequence: (1) developing an individual disorder model and treatment rationale, (2) individual diagnostics and problem definition, (3) implementation, and (4) consolidation, with the length of each part adapted to the patient.

Homework was reviewed at the start of each session, and patients received weekly feedback on their progress based on their SCED plots.

Feedback

Each week during therapy, therapists received a visual feedback report displaying the patient's target item over time. The report included a line plot depicting repeated measurements of the target variable across the different study phases, with phase transitions visually marked to facilitate interpretation of change over time. In addition, quantitative feedback regarding treatment effects was provided using the TAU-BC statistic and the Bayes Factor to support evaluation of changes associated with the intervention (de Vries et al., 2015; Tarlow, 2017). Therapists were instructed to review and discuss the feedback with patients at the beginning of each therapy session and to integrate the results into the ongoing therapeutic process.

Procedural and treatment fidelity

Before treatment, all study therapists completed a workshop on SCED and personspecific network analysis. During therapy, therapists received supervision at least every fourth session. Treatment fidelity was assessed with a module-specific adherence checklist derived from the treatment manual and expert review (e.g., Leeuw et al., 2009), covering unique, permitted, and prohibited elements of each module as well as whether EMA feedback and the treatment rationale were discussed (see S1.2). Two independent raters not involved in treatment delivery rated four video-recorded sessions per patient spanning all therapy phases and judged whether the assigned module had been delivered as intended (adherent vs. nonadherent; interrater reliability: Cohen's κ; J. Cohen, 1960)). In addition, therapists documented the interventions used after each session (self-rated fidelity).

Analyses

Analyses followed the published study protocol (V. E. Hofmann et al., 2024), which specified visual analysis, baseline-corrected Tau, and Hedges' g for the evaluation of individual treatment effects. Within-person regression models and a multilevel model of average treatment effects were conducted in addition and should be considered exploratory. Further exploratory analyses not specified in the protocol (Bayes factors and comparisons of pre-and post-treatment networks) and descriptive results for secondary outcomes are reported in the Supplementary Material (S1.5, S1.7, S1.8, S2.5, S2.6, S2.7). The qualitative analysis was preregistered separately (V. E. Hofmann, Glombiewski, & Scholten, 2026).

We report how we determined our sample size, all data exclusions, all manipulations, and all measures in the study. All analyses were conducted in R (R Development Core Team, 2025; version 4.6.0) within RStudio (Posit, 2025; version 2026.04.0+526). Several packages for data management, visualization, and statistical analysis (see S1.10). Missing observations and exclusions were handled separately for each analysis as described below.

Network-based treatment assignment

Descriptive statistics (mean, median, standard deviation) were calculated for each EMA variable. Positively valenced items (e.g., positive affect, acceptance) were recoded where necessary to ensure consistent directionality across variables. Variable selection followed predefined criteria to improve robustness of individual network estimation.

Individual-level temporal (lag-1) and residual contemporaneous networks were estimated (Epskamp et al., 2018). Consistent with recommendations for short assessment periods, data were not detrended. Treatment target selection was based on the DATA-IN algorithm (see S1.4; Rubel et al., 2018).

Preregistered treatment effects

Visual analysis. Time-series plots of the target variable across baseline and intervention phases were inspected for changes in level and trend between phases (Lane & Gast, 2014).

Single-case effect estimation.

Treatment effects on the individualized target variable and on EMA-assessed disability were quantified using baseline-corrected Tau (BC-Tau; Tarlow, 2017) and Hedges' g (Carlin & Costello, 2021). BC-Tau is a non-parametric effect size that estimates the proportion of non-overlapping data between baseline and intervention phases while accounting for baseline trends and ranges from -1 to +1 (Tarlow, 2017). The baseline trend was first tested using Kendall's rank correlation. If the trend was significant (p < .05), it was removed using Theil-Sen regression before Tau was computed; otherwise, Tau was computed without correction. Hedges' g was calculated as the standardized mean difference between the baseline (C) and intervention (D) phases, using the pooled within-case standard deviation and a small-sample bias correction (Hedges, 1981).

Exploratory treatment Within-person regressions.

To estimate phase-related change for the EMA-assessed target variable and disability respectively within participants, separate regression models were fitted using ordinary least squares, without modeling autocorrelation (see S1.6).

Multi-level model. Linear mixed-effects models were estimated to estimate average treatment effects across participants while accounting for repeated measurements nested within individuals. The multilevel model was estimated for EMA-assessed disability only, as the individualized target variables differed across participants and could not be pooled (see S1.6).

Evaluation of usability and acceptability

Usability outcomes were summarized descriptively. Qualitative data were analyzed using an evaluative content analysis approach (Kuckartz & Rädiker, 2022) as outlined in the see S1.9.

Transparency and openness

All data, analysis code, and research materials are available in the Open Science Framework [Project link: https://osf.io/9tuqr/overview?view_only=2a908f5aa87143809199f37339534ef1; Data, code, and material related to this publication: https://osf.io/tsdc8/overview?view_only=4e564f92f03e40b2ad5e37c678fe70b0].

Results

Participants

A total of N = 29 participants were recruited between November 2023 and June 2024through the outpatient clinic's waiting list, referrals from healthcare providers, and public advertisements. Of these, N = 15 participants were enrolled in the study. During the study, N = 3 participants discontinued participation, resulting in a final sample of N = 12 participants included in the analyses. Data collection was completed in August 2025. A detailed overview of participant flow is provided in the Supplementary Material S2.1.

The final sample consisted of N = 12 participants with chronic primary pain (see S2.2 Sociodemographic characteristics of the patients, Table S2). Participants had a mean age of M = 60.25 years (SD = 7.2, range = 54-80). All participants met criteria for chronic primary pain (ICD-10: F45.41). Comorbid mental disorders were present in n = 5 participants, with the most frequent diagnoses being depressive disorders (n = 5).

Therapists had a mean age of 32.3 years (range: 26-39) and were predominantly female (n = 7), with two male therapists. Two therapists were fully licensed, while seven were in clinical training. Most had between 0 and 5 years of professional experience (n = 7), with three reporting 6-10 years and one reporting 11-15 years of experience. Three therapists treated two patients each.

Feasibility

Number of observations and compliance.

Overall, the assessment protocol resulted in 3580 observations with an average of 298 (Min: 188; Max: 376) observations per person.

Compliance ranged from 24 -100% with high variations across phases (A: 73-98%; B: 55-100%; C: 50-100%; D: 45-100%; second intensive EMA phase: 24-97%). Overall, at least seven patients reached the pre-defined criterion of 80% compliance in every phase, with a maximum of 11 patients reaching 80% in the diagnostic phase and in the post-treatment EMA phase.

Module assignment.

In the clinical case conceptualization, conducted without knowledge of the network results, the clinical team identified up to three suitable modules per patient. These overlapped with at least one of the two algorithm-recommended modules in 6 of 13 cases (46%) and with both in two cases. The clinical team's first choice matched an algorithm-recommended module in only two cases. The primary recommendation was adopted in 12 cases, and the alternative module was chosen in 1 case because adverse effects were anticipated. No module was reassigned during treatment.

Treatment fidelity.

Interrater agreement for the global adherence judgment was substantial (Cohen's κ = .63). Both raters judged 10 of 12 treatments as adherent; one treatment was rated adherent by only one rater, and one was rated non-adherent by both.

Therapists self-reported deviations from the assigned module in three treatments. Agreement between external ratings and therapists' self-reports was slight (Fleiss' κ = .16).

Preliminary treatment effects

Preregistered treatment effects

Visual analysis indicated consistent improvement in level and trend in six participants, mixed patterns in three, and deterioration in three (see S2.3). Baseline-Corrected TAU did not indicate significant change for any participant (Table 1). Consistent with the BC-Tau results, Hedges' g varied in direction and magnitude (Table 1). For the target items, g ranged from -1.36 to 1.50, with lower scores during the intervention phase in eight participants and higher scores in four; for disability, g ranged from -1.44 to 0.47, with lower scores in ten participants. Given the short baseline phases (3-12 observations), these estimates are imprecise and are reported descriptively.

Exploratory treatment effects

Within-person regressions of the target item showed lower scores during the intervention phase than during baseline for eight participants and higher scores for four (Table 1). One participant improved significantly (P1: β₁ = -0.79, 95% CI [-1.34, -0.24]), and two deteriorated significantly (P2: β₁ = 1.35, 95% CI [0.28, 2.41]; P4: β₁ = 2.75, 95% CI [1.15, 4.36]).

Within-person regression analyses of the disability item showed lower scores during

the intervention phase in ten participants and higher in two (P8, P10). Decreases were significant for two participants (P1: β₁ = -0.79, 95% CI [-1.34, -0.24]; P4: β₁ = -1.43, 95% CI [-2.31, -0.56]). See Table S3 in die Supplementary Material (S2.4).

The random-intercept model showed an average decrease in EMA-assessed disability of 𝛽1 = -0.45 points on the [0-10] scale (SE = 0.21, t(611.35) = -2.15, p = .03; Satterthwaiteapproximated df). Adding random slopes did not improve model fit (χ²(2) = 2.10, p = .35); given the small number of participants, this does not indicate homogeneous treatment response.

---Please insert Table 1 here ---

Acceptability

Usability

Patients and therapists rated the usability of the m-Path app and of the presentation of results (network and feedback plots) on the System Usability Scale (SUS; range 0-100). The app received high ratings from patients (M = 85.19, SD = 16.81) and above-average ratings from therapists (M = 69.44, SD = 22.90). The presentation of results was rated lower, particularly by therapists (patients: M = 79.23, SD = 18.21; therapists: M = 53.06, SD = 16.29). On the User Experience Questionnaire (UEQ-S; range -3 to 3), hedonic quality was positive for patients (M = 1.54, SD = 1.29) and therapists (M = 1.00, SD = 0.84). Pragmatic quality was lower and exceeded the threshold for a positive evaluation (> 0.8; Schrepp et al., 2017) among patients (M = 0.87, SD = 0.59) but not among therapists (M = 0.64, SD = 0.64).

Qualitative results

Rater agreement for assigning paraphrases to research questions was good (patients: κ = .76; therapists: κ = .83). The complete category system with example quotes is provided in the Supplementary Material S2.8.

Use of data in therapy.

Patients and therapists described POINT-Pain as a structured, yet flexible framework integrated into ongoing psychotherapy. Both groups experienced the data visualizations as orientation that helped maintain therapeutic focus and supported treatment decisions. Network and feedback plots were discussed in sessions and served as a starting point for reflecting on symptom relations and treatment targets; therapists used the network to explain treatment rationales, and patients appreciated that planning was guided by data rather than intuition alone. Patients also engaged with their data between sessions.

Deviations from the protocol, such as irregular use of feedback, were attributed to practical barriers, forgetfulness, or difficulties understanding the visualizations.

Perceived effects and mechanisms. Perceived effects ranged from reduced symptoms and improved functioning to a better understanding of pain and increased self-awareness.

However, some participants reported little or no change (e.g., Therapist: "I don't really have much hope that much has changed." ; Patient: "The pain is still there." ; Negative side effects and non-response), and some patients experienced the frequent assessments as burdensome reminders of their pain. Both groups attributed change to factors such as the therapeutic relationship, to the structured, goal-oriented treatment process, and to repeated selfobservation and personalized feedback, which increased awareness of everyday patterns and created opportunities for reflection. For example, one therapist described "refram[ing] it and manag [ing] to get the curve after all," while a patient explained that therapy focused on improving interpersonal functioning. Some therapists could not attribute change to a specific component, and patients also named contextual factors such as concurrent physiotherapy.

Therapists additionally reported professional learning, including a more structured and goaloriented approach. For example, one therapist stated, "For me, self-compassion hadn't even crossed my mind before," while another reflected, "I learned a lot about pain." Facilitators and barriers. Therapists described facilitators and barriers in similar measure, whereas patients reported more barriers. Experiences varied considerably between participants and often reflected opposite poles of the same issue, such as "Formulating idiographic items was easy." and "I realized that I didn't have many ideas either, so only very lackluster ideas emerged, which were harder to put into words [idiographic items]."

Participants emphasized understandable item wording, addressing initial concerns, technical support, training and supervision, and a balance between structure and flexibility (see S2.9).

Discussion

This proof-of-concept study examined how Ecological Momentary Assessment (EMA) based monitoring, person-specific networks, and modular evidence-based interventions can be integrated into data-informed personalized treatment for chronic pain, and how such an approach functions in clinical practice. The approach proved largely feasible: patients provided a mean of 298 observations, and treatments were mostly delivered as intended, although three of 15 patients discontinued and not all participants met the compliance criterion in every phase. Preliminary effects were mixed. Disability decreased on average, but individual trajectories ranged from improvement to deterioration. Patients and therapists primarily valued the self-monitoring and structured, data-informed reflection the approach enabled. At the same time, participant burden, the interpretability of the network output, and the balance between structure and flexibility emerged as areas for improvement.

Designing this approach highlighted several unresolved questions along the individual-level clinical decision-making pathway (Scholten & Glombiewski, 2025). We discuss these along the three steps of the framework: observing, deciding, and intervening.

Observing: Ecological Momentary Assessment and Self-Monitoring

Patients experienced the intensive EMA phase as burdensome yet missed it once assessments were reduced to three per week. This suggests a non-linear trade-off between burden and perceived informational value. Rather than a universal sampling rate, the findings support flexible, hybrid monitoring architectures that combine measurement bursts to capture short-term dynamics (Andresen et al., 2025), moderate-frequency monitoring to inform case formulation, and lower-frequency Routine Outcome Monitoring (ROM) to track progress (Barkham et al., 2023), with assessment design treated as an iterative clinical decision.

Beyond data collection, patients and therapists described self-monitoring as an active part of therapy that increased awareness of everyday patterns and brought daily-life experiences into sessions. EMA may thus function not only as assessment infrastructure but as a processsupporting intervention element (Bos et al., 2022; Piccirillo et al., 2026; Scholten et al., 2026).

Deciding: Data-Informed Clinical Reasoning and Treatment Selection

The most direct evidence on how network-based recommendations function in practice comes from comparing them with clinical judgment. The modules selected by the clinical team without knowledge of the network results overlapped with the algorithmic recommendations in fewer than half of the cases, and the team's first choice matched an algorithm-recommended module in only two cases. The networks thus did not merely confirm clinicians' formulations but introduced a different perspective. Whether this perspective was more accurate cannot be determined from the present data. Disagreement may stem from target selection as well as from the matching matrix, which, for instance, assigned the same modules whenever disability was the target, whereas clinicians chose different modules in each of these cases. Based on these findings, we have revised the matching matrix (V. E. Hofmann, Glombiewski, & Scholten, 2026). Because the decision rule permitted deviations only when adverse effects were anticipated, the primary recommendation was adopted in 12 of 13 cases, and treatment selection was therefore largely algorithm-driven. The value of the Algorithm-Guided Intervention Seletion Team (AGIST) thus lay less in changing decisions than in the reflection it prompted. Therapists described it as a particularly valuable space for reflecting on decisions, clarifying patient aims, and making intervention choices transparent, and both groups used the networks to explain treatment rationales. At the same time, therapists rated the usability of the network output below average, some participants found the visualizations difficult to understand, and both patients and therapists raised ethical concerns about data-driven decisions. Together, these findings suggest that the clinical value of the network approach does not depend on statistical accuracy alone. Its primary contribution may lie in providing a structured language for representing interactions between symptoms, experiences, and behaviors that supports collaborative case conceptualization. In this sense, simplified or qualitatively derived perceived causal networks may be sufficient, or in some cases preferable (Vogel et al., 2025), and hybrid approaches combining patient knowledge, clinical expertise, and empirical data may strengthen this translational step (Burger et al., 2022; Scholten, Klintwall, et al., 2025).

The study also underscored the role of patient goals. Monitoring personally defined goals provides a stable reference point for evaluating progress (Cooper & Xu, 2023; Weisz et al., 2011), whereas dynamically assessed variables can be understood as potential mechanisms influencing goal attainment. Goals, mechanisms, and interventions may thus be viewed as complementary levels of clinical reasoning (the destination, the terrain, and the route) rather than a linear sequence.

Intervening: Structured Treatment Delivery and Process Evaluation

Delivering one selected module was experienced as focused, facilitating patients' transfer of coping strategies into daily life and therapists' procedural competence. However, patients perceived the module as too long, therapists experienced limited flexibility, and both noted conceptual overlap between modules. The slight agreement between external fidelity ratings and therapists' self-reports suggests that therapists may have adapted the procedure more than they recognized or reported, a further indication of the tension between structure and flexibility. Future research should examine the specific and shared effects of modules, their optimal dosage, and sequencing (Mulder et al., 2017).

The preliminary effects should be interpreted in this light. Disability decreased on average, but no individual BC-Tau estimate was significant, and two participants deteriorated significantly on their target. With baselines of only 3-12 observations, individual-level analyses had limited power and precision, so nonsignificant results do not indicate an absence of change. The deteriorations nevertheless warrant attentionP2 experienced several stressful life events during treatment that therapy could not sufficiently address. For P4, post hoc case discussions in the study team suggested that the increase in ignoring pain was functional for this patient as it enabled engagement in valued activities, which the item formulation did not capture. This illustrates that the direction of change on a single EMA item cannot be interpreted without clinical context. In addition, patients described meaningful changes, such as new perspectives and coping strategies, that the outcome measures did not capture (De Smet et al., 2020), and therapists expressed a need for clearer guidelines for translating weekly SCED feedback into clinical decisions, comparable to established ROM systems (e.g., Barkham et al., 2023; Lutz et al., 2022).

Strengths and limitations

Strengths of the study include its mixed-methods design combining patient and therapist perspectives, a published protocol, external fidelity ratings, and the blinded clinical case conceptualization, which allowed a direct comparison of clinical and algorithmic treatment selection.

Several limitations concern the approach itself. Person-specific networks remain methodologically contested with regard to the reliability of time-varying data, construct validity, the stability of estimates, and the use of centrality indices for target selection (Bringmann, 2024; Cusack et al., 2025; Scholten et al., 2024; Zhang et al., 2025)

Clinical implications

Personalized psychotherapy may benefit from being conceptualized not as the application of isolated individualized techniques but as a recursive cycle in which observation informs decisions, decisions guide interventions, and interventions generate new data that refine subsequent understanding. From this perspective, the clinical value of data-informed personalization may lie less in algorithmic precision than in its capacity to raise awareness, structure reflection, enhance shared understanding, and support the iterative updating of clinicians' and patients' beliefs about the therapeutic process (Herzog et al., 2023). Future developments should therefore focus not only on methodological sophistication but also on the interpretability, flexibility, and clinical usability of personalized psychotherapy frameworks in routine care.

Declaration of generative AI and AI-assisted technologies in the manuscript preparation process

During the preparation of this work the authors used ChatGPT (OpenAI, 2026), DeepL (DeepL SE, 2026), and Claude (Opus 5.5; Anthropic, 2026) to support the translation of text included in the manuscript, as well as for language editing and stylistic refinements. ChatGPT and Claude were additionally used to assist with the development and refinement of coding for statistical analyses and refine Figure 1 and 2. All outputs generated with these tools were edited, reviewed, and verified by the authors, who take full responsibility for the content of the manuscript.

: This work was supported by the WiPP e.V. (University Outpatient Clinic).

The authors declare no competing interests.

, and the networks were not updated during treatment. Moreover, the networks captured hour-to-hour dynamics (lag-1 at six assessments per day), whereas treatment progress was evaluated week to week; processes at one time scale do not necessarily drive change at another. It also remains unclear which components of the framework (EMA, network modeling, intervention selection, or SCED-based feedback) contributed to the observed changes, or whether benefits emerge from their combination. Finally, the framework focuses on intra-individual dynamics, whereas change also unfolds within biological, social, and environmental contexts (von Klipstein et al., 2025). Methodological limitations include a moderate risk of bias (RoBiNT: 18 of 30 points; V. E. Hofmann et al., 2024) due to nonconcurrent baselines, incomplete blinding, and the absence of systematic replication across phases, which limits causal inference. Because of limited resources, members of the research team served as therapists in four cases, which may have compromised blinding and treatment fidelity. Autocorrelation was not modeled in the regression and multilevel analyses, and the network invariance test could not be interpreted for seven of twelve participants. Finally, the small sample, three dropouts, and the idiographic nature of the data limit generalizability. Future studies should separate clinical and research roles, use replication-focused designs, and include more diverse samples.
